This theorem originally appears in
[
2
]
. However, the proof there is combinatorial in nature. Our proof uses the material developed in
Section
2
, leveraging the fact that
\infty
-categories of spans have natural incarnations as (complete) Segal spaces.

Before we get to the meat of the proof, however, we have to prove some preliminary results.

We also note a variant, the proof of which is similar but easier.

Theorem 3.3.3
.

Let
p\colon(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\to(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})
be a functor between adequate triples such that
p\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is an inner fibration which satisfies the following conditions.

1.

The subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
consists of all
p
-cartesian morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
; that is, an
n
-simplex in
\mathcal{C}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
if and only if each
1
-simplex it contains is
p
-cartesian.

2.

The map
p^{\dagger}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}\to\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}
is a cartesian fibration.

3.

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -15.7 -22.24 L 17.4 -22.24}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-11.14975pt}{-16.07143pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 3.32 C 2.35 1.33 1.18 0.39 0 0 C 1.18 -0.39 2.35 -1.33 2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.77596pt}{-16.07143pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.56888pt}{-12.35756pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

in
\mathcal{C}
where
g
and
g^{\prime}
belong to
\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
, and
f
belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{\dagger}
. Further suppose that
f
is
p
-cocartesian. Then
f^{\prime}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{\dagger}
, and is both
p
-cocartesian and
p_{\dagger}
-cocartesian.

Then spans of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

are cocartesian, where
g
is
p^{\dagger}
-cartesian and
f
is
p
-cocartesian.

3.4
Housekeeping

In this section we recall some basic facts we will need in the course of our proof of the main theorem. It is recommended to skip this section on first reading, and refer back to the results as necessary.

3.4.1
Isofibrations

We will need some results about isofibrations, sometimes called categorical fibrations. These are standard, and proofs can be found in
[
7
]
.

Proposition 3.4.1
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a map between quasicategories. The following are equivalent:

•

The map
f
is an isofibration.

•

The map
f
is an inner fibration, and each equivalence in
\mathcal{D}
has a lift in
\mathcal{C}
which is an equivalence.

We will denote by
\mathrm{Fun}(-,-)
the internal hom on
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}
.

Proposition 3.4.2
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be an isofibration between quasicategories, and let
i\colon A\hookrightarrow B
be an inclusion of simplicial sets. Then the map

\mathrm{Fun}(B,\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{Fun}(A,\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Fun}(A,\mathcal{D})}\mathrm{Fun}(B,\mathcal{D})

is an isofibration.

We will denote by
(-)^{\simeq}
the
core
functor, i.e. the functor which takes a simplicial set
X
to the largest Kan complex it contains.

Proposition 3.4.3
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be an isofibration of simplicial sets. Then

f^{\simeq}\colon X^{\simeq}\to Y^{\simeq}

is a Kan fibration.

We will denote the core of the functor
\mathrm{Fun}(-,-)
by
\mathrm{Map}(-,-)
. That is, for simplicial sets
A
and
B
, we have

\mathrm{Map}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{Fun}(A,B)^{\simeq}.

Corollary 3.4.4
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be an isofibration between quasicategories, and let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an inclusion of simplicial sets. Then the map

\mathrm{Map}(B,\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{Map}(A,\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(A,\mathcal{D})}\mathrm{Map}(B,\mathcal{D})

is a Kan fibration.

3.4.2
Connected components

Our proof will rely heavily on the fact that the many properties that morphisms can have (ingressive, egressive,
p
-cocartesian, etc.) are well-behaved with respect to the homotopical structure of the complete Segal spaces in which they live; for example, if a morphism is
p
-cocartesian, then every morphism in its path component is
p
-cocartesian.

Let
X^{\prime}
and
X
be simplicial sets, and let
f\colon X^{\prime}\hookrightarrow X
be an inclusion. We will say that
f
is an
inclusion of connected components
if for all simplices
\sigma\in X
, if
\sigma
has any vertex in common with
X^{\prime}
, then
\sigma
is wholly contained in
X^{\prime}
.

Lemma 3.4.5
.

Let
A
be a simplicial set such that between any two vertices
x
and
y
of
A
there exists a finite zig-zag of 1-simplices of
A
connecting
x
to
y
. Let
A_{0}\subseteq
A be a nonempty simplicial subset of
A
. Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\hookrightarrow X
be a morphism between simplicial sets which is an inclusion of connected components. Then the following dashed lift always exists.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Proof.

We have a map
f\colon A\to X
; in order to construct our dashed lift, it suffices to show that under the above assumptions,
f
takes every simplex of
A
to a simplex which belongs to
X^{\prime}
. To this end, let
\sigma\in A
be a simplex. There exists a finite zig-zag of
1
-simplices connecting some vertex of
\sigma
to a vertex
\alpha
of
A_{0}
; under
f
, this is mapped to a zig-zag of
1
-simplices connecting
f(\alpha)
to
f(\sigma)
. The vertex
f(\alpha)
belongs to
X^{\prime}
; proceeding inductively, we find that the image of every 1-simplex belonging to the zig-zag also belongs to
X^{\prime}
, and that
f(\sigma)
therefore also belongs to
X^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 3.4.6
.

Given any commuting square of simplicial sets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with monomorphisms as marked, where
i
is an inclusion of connected components between Kan complexes and
f
is a Kan fibration, the map
f^{\prime}
is a Kan fibration.

Furthermore, if
f
is a trivial fibration and
f^{\prime}
is surjective on vertices, then
f^{\prime}
is a trivial fibration.

Proof.

First, we show that if
f
is a Kan fibration, then
f^{\prime}
is a Kan fibration. We need to show that a dashed lift below exists for each
n\geq 1
and each
0\leq i\leq n
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{n}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

We can always solve our outer lifting problem, and by
Lemma
3.4.5
, our lift factors through
X^{\prime}
.

Now suppose that
f
is a trivial Kan fibration. The logic above says that
f^{\prime}
has the right lifting property with respect to
\partial\Delta^{n}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{n}
for
n\geq 1
; the right lifting property with respect to
\partial\Delta^{0}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{0}
is equivalent to the surjectivity of
f^{\prime}
on vertices.
∎

3.4.3
Contractibility

We will need at certain points to show that various spaces of lifts are contractible. These are mostly common-sense results, but it will be helpful to have them written down somewhere so we can refer to them later.

Lemma 3.4.7
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be an inner fibration between quasicategories. Then for any commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.32309pt}{-15.33589pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

the fiber
F
in the pullback square below is contractible.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{2},\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\Lambda^{2}_{1},\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\Lambda^{2}_{1},\mathcal{D})}\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{2},\mathcal{D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha,f\alpha,\beta)}

Proof.

The right-hand map is a trivial Kan fibration.
∎

One should interpret this as telling us that given a
\Lambda^{2}_{2}
-horn
\alpha
in
\mathcal{C}
lying over a 2-simplex
\beta
in
\mathcal{D}
, the space of fillings of
\alpha
lying over
\beta
is contractible. We will need a pantheon of similar results.

Lemma 3.4.8
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be an inner fibration between quasicategories. Let
e
be any
f
-cartesian edge. Then for any square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{2}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

such that
\alpha|_{\{1,2\}}=e
, the fiber
F
in the pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{2},\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\Lambda^{2}_{2},\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\Lambda^{2}_{1},\mathcal{D})}\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{2},\mathcal{D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha,f\alpha,\beta)}

(5)

is contractible.

Proof.

Denote
f(e)=\bar{e}
. Define a marking
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{D}
containing all degenerate edges and
\bar{e}
. Define a marking
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{C}
containing the
f
-cocartesian lifts of the edges in
\mathcal{E}
. By
[
6
, Prop. 3.1.1.6]
, the map
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{E}}\to\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
has the right-lifting property with respect to all cocartesian-marked anodyne morphisms. It follows that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\sharp}((\Delta^{2})^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{E}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\sharp}((\Lambda^{2}_{2})^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{E}})\times_{\mathrm{Fun}^{\sharp}((\Lambda^{2}_{1})^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}})}\mathrm{Fun}^{\sharp}((\Delta^{2})^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}})}

is a trivial Kan fibration; the map on the right-hand side of
Diagram
5
is the core of this map, and is thus also a trivial Kan fibration.
∎

Lemma 3.4.9
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\to(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})
be an inner fibration between triples satisfying the conditions of
Theorem
3.3.2
. Denote by
\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
the full simplicial subset of
\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
on edges
\Delta^{1}\to\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
which are both
p
-cocartesian and
p^{\dagger}
-cocartesian. Then the fibers of the map

\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\to\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{\{0\}},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{\{0\}},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})}\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})

are contractible.

Proof.

Denote by
\mathrm{Map}^{\prime\prime}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
the full simplicial subset of
\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
on edges which are
p^{\dagger}
-cartesian. Then we have a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\prime\prime}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{\{0\}},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{\{0\}},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})}\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{\{0\}},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{\{0\}},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})}\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})}

in which the top map is an inclusion of connected components, the right map is a trivial Kan fibration, and the left map is surjective on vertices. Thus, the left map is a trivial Kan fibration by
Corollary
3.4.6

∎

3.5
Proof of main theorem

Our goal is now to prove
Theorem
3.3.2
. As in the statement of the theorem, we fix a functor of triples
p\colon(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\to(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})
giving us a functor of quasicategories

\pi\colon\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}).

We wish to show that the following hold.

1.

The map
\pi
is an inner fibration.

2.

Morphsims of the form

(6)

are
\pi
-cocartesian, where
\phi
is egressive and
p_{\dagger}
-cartesian, and
\psi
is ingressive and
p
-cocartesian.

The way forward is clear: the functor
\pi
is the zeroth row of a functor of Segal spaces

\pi^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{Span}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\to\mathbf{Span}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}).

The results of
Section
2
therefore guarantee the following.

1.

By
Corollary
2.1.8
, in order to show that
\pi
is an inner fibration, it suffices to show that
\pi^{\prime}
is a Reedy fibration.

2.

In order to show that morphisms of the required form are
\pi
-cocartesian, it will suffice to show by
Corollary
2.4.15
that morphisms of this form are
\pi^{\prime}
-cocartesian.

3.5.1
The map
\pi
is an inner fibration

We begin by checking that the map
\pi
is an inner fibration. As stated above, we do this by checking that
\pi^{\prime}
is a Reedy fibration. This follows from the following useful fact, implied by the first assumption of
Theorem
3.3.2
.

Lemma 3.5.1
.

Let
p\colon(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\to(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})
be a functor of triples satisfying the conditions of
Theorem
3.3.2
. Then the map
p\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is an isofibration.

Proof.

By assumption,
p
is an inner fibration and
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are quasicategories, so it suffices by
Proposition
3.4.1
to show that
p
admits lifts of equivalences. Each equivalence in
\mathcal{D}
is in particular ingressive, and hence admits a
p
-cocartesian lift by the assumptions of
Theorem
3.3.2
; these are automatically equivalences by
[
6
, Prop. 2.4.1.5]
.
∎

Proposition 3.5.2
.

For any functor of adequate triples
p\colon(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\to(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})
, the map

\pi^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{Span}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\to\mathbf{Span}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})

is a Reedy fibration.

Proof.

Consider the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n}),\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n}),\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(\partial\Delta^{n}),\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(\partial\Delta^{n}),\mathcal{D})}\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n}),\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{sd}(\partial\Delta^{n}),\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{sd}(\partial\Delta^{n}),\mathcal{D})}\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n}),\mathcal{D})}
.

The right-hand map is a Kan fibration by
Corollary
3.4.4
(because
\mathrm{sd}
preserves monomorphisms), and the top map is an inclusion of connected components, so
Corollary
3.4.6
implies that the left-hand map is a Kan fibration, which is what we needed to show.
∎

Corollary 3.5.3
.

The map
\pi
is an inner fibration

Proof.

Corollary
2.1.8
.
∎

3.5.2
Cocartesian morphisms have the promised form

We are now ready to begin in earnest our proof that morphisms of the form
Equation
6
are
\pi^{\prime}
-cocartesian. Morally, this result should not be surprising. We should think of the homotopy pullback condition defining cocartesian morphisms (
Definition
2.4.1
) as telling us that we can fill relative
\Lambda^{2}_{2}
-horns in
\pi^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{Span}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\to\mathbf{Span}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})
, and that such fillings are unique up to contractible choice. Finding a filling

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{2}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Span}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Span}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}

is equivalent finding a filling

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda^{2}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

(such that the filling is ambigressive and the necessary square is pullback); the conditions on
p
guarantee us that we can perform this filling in a series of steps pictured in
Figure
1
, each of which is unique up to contractible choice.

In fact, our proof mainly consists of making this idea rigorous. To this end, we first introduce some notation. Let
p\colon(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\to(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})
be a functor of triples.

•

We will denote
p
-cartesian morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
with a circle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}

•

We will denote
p
-cocartesian morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
with a bullet:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}

•

We will denote
p^{\dagger}
-cartesian morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
with a triangle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartriangleright}

•

We will denote
p_{\dagger}
-cocartesian morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
with a filled triangle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\blacktriangleright}

Thus, an ingressive morphism which is both
p
-cocartesian and
p_{\dagger}
-cocartesian will be denoted by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\blacktriangleright\bullet}

Our proof will rest on the factorization
\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda^{2}_{0})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{2})
pictured in
Figure
1
. Denote the underlying factorization of simplicial sets by

A_{1}\overset{i_{1}}{\hookrightarrow}A_{2}\overset{i_{2}}{\hookrightarrow}A_{3}\overset{i_{3}}{\hookrightarrow}A_{4}\overset{i_{4}}{\hookrightarrow}A_{5}\overset{i_{5}}{\hookrightarrow}A_{6}

Figure 1:
A factorization of the inclusion
\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda^{2}_{0})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{2})
, where certain morphisms have been labelled.

For each of the
A_{i}
above, denote by

\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{i},\mathcal{C})\subseteq\mathrm{Map}(A_{i},\mathcal{C})

(7)

the full simplicial subset on those functors
A_{i}\to\mathcal{C}
which respect each labelling in
Figure
1
:

•

ingressive

•

egressive

•

p
-cartesian

•

p
-cocartesian

•

p^{\dagger}
-cartesian

•

p_{\dagger}
-cocartesian

Note that because equivalences in
\mathcal{C}
belong to each of these classes of morphisms, the inclusion in
Equation
7
is an inclusion of connected components.

Similarly, we will denote by
\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{i},\mathcal{D})
the full simplicial subset on functors which respect the ingressive and egressive labellings.

Lemma 3.5.4
.

The square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{6},\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{1},\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{6},\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{1},\mathcal{D})}

is homotopy pullback.

Figure
1
gives us a factorization of the above square into five squares

each corresponding to one of the inclusions
i_{k}
. We will be done if we can show that each of these squares is homotopy pullback. It suffices to show that for each
k
, the map

j_{k}\colon\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{k+1},\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{k},\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{k+1},\mathcal{D})}\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{k},\mathcal{D})

is a weak equivalence. First, we show that each of these maps is a Kan fibration. To see this, consider the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{k+1},\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(A_{k+1},\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{k},\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{k+1},\mathcal{D})}\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{k},\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(A_{k},\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(A_{k+1},\mathcal{D})}\mathrm{Map}(A_{k},\mathcal{D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{k}}
.

The right-hand map is a Kan fibration because of
Corollary
3.4.4
, and we have already seen that the top map is an inclusion of connected componenents. Hence each
j_{k}
is a Kan fibration by
Corollary
3.4.6
. Thus, in order to show that each
j_{k}
is a weak equivalence, it suffices to show that the fibers are contractible.

First consider the case
k=1
. For any map
\alpha
below, consider the following pullback square.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The fiber
F_{\alpha}
over
\alpha
is the space of ways of completing a diagram of shape
A_{0}
in
\mathcal{C}
to a diagram of shape
A_{1}
in
\mathcal{C}
given a diagram of shape
A_{1}
in
\mathcal{D}
. This is the space of ways of filling
\Lambda^{2}_{2}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{2}
in
\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
lying over a
2
-simplex in
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}
. This is contractible by
Lemma
3.4.8
.

The case
k=2
is similar, using
Lemma
3.4.7
.

The case
k=3
is similar, using
Lemma
3.4.9
.

The cases
k=4
and
k=5
use the dual to
Lemma
3.4.8
.
∎

We are now ready to show that morphisms of the form
Diagram
6
are
\pi^{\prime}
-cocartesian.

Proposition 3.5.5
.

For any morphism
e
of the form given in
Equation
6
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{2}),\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\{0,1\}}),\mathcal{C})}\{e\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda^{2}_{0}),\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\{0,1\}}),\mathcal{C})}\{e\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{2}),\mathcal{D})\times_{\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\{0,1\}}),\mathcal{D})}\{\pi e\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda^{2}_{0}),\mathcal{D})\times_{\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\{0,1\}}),\mathcal{D})}\{\pi e\}}

is homotopy pullback.

Proof.

This square factors horizontally into the two squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{2}),\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\{0,1\}}),\mathcal{C})}\{e\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{6},\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\{0,1\}}),\mathcal{C})}\{e\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{2}),\mathcal{D})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\{0,1\}}),\mathcal{D})}\{\pi e\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{6},\mathcal{D})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\{0,1\}}),\mathcal{C})}\{\pi e\}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{6},\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\{0,1\}}),\mathcal{C})}\{e\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{1},\mathcal{C})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\{0,1\}}),\mathcal{C})}\{e\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{6},\mathcal{D})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\{0,1\}}),\mathcal{C})}\{\pi e\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{1},\mathcal{D})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\{0,1\}}),\mathcal{C})}\{\pi e\}}
.

The first is homotopy pullback because the bottom map is an inclusion of connected components, and the second condition of
Theorem
3.3.2
guarantees that the fiber over an ambigressive
\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{2})\to\mathcal{D}
belonging to
\mathrm{Map}^{\prime}(A_{6},\mathcal{D})
whose restriction to
\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\{0,1\}})
is
\pi e
is precisely an ambigressive Cartesian functor
\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{2})\to\mathcal{C}
whose restriction to
\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\{0,1\}})
is
e
.

That the second is homotopy pullback follows immediately from
Lemma
3.5.4
.
∎

This proves
Theorem
3.3.2
.

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1
A brief list of model structures

The category
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}
carries two model structures of which we will make frequent use:

•

The
Kan
model structure, which has the following description.

\circ

The fibrations are the Kan fibrations.

\circ

The cofibrations are the monomorphisms.

\circ

The weak equivalences are weak homotopy equivalences.

•

The
Joyal
model structure. We will not give a complete description, referring the reader to
[
6
, Sec. 2.2.5]
. We will make use of the following properties.

\circ

The cofibrations are monomorphisms.

\circ

The fibrant objects are quasicategories, and the fibrations between fibrant objects are isofibrations, i.e. inner fibrations with lifts of equivalences (cf.
[
6
, Cor. 2.6.5]
).

The category
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
has a Reedy structure, which gives us, together with the Kan model structure, a model structure on the category
\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta})=\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta^{2}}
with the following properties.

•

The cofibrations are monomorphisms.

•

The weak equivalences are level-wise weak homotopy equivalences.

•

The fibrations are
Reedy fibrations
(
Definition
2.1.6
).

This model category has two important left Bousfeld localizations which we will need.

•

The
Segal space model structure
on
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta^{2}}
is the left Bousfeld localization of the Reedy model structure at the set of spine inclusions

S=\left\{\left.\Delta^{\{0,1\}}\amalg_{\Delta^{\{1\}}}\Delta^{\{1,2\}}\amalg_{\Delta^{\{2\}}}\cdots\amalg_{\Delta^{\{n-1\}}}\Delta^{\{n-1,n\}}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{n}\ \right|\ n\geq 2\right\},

which has the following properties.

\circ

The cofibrations are the monomorphisms.

\circ

The fibrant objects are Segal spaces.

\circ

Every Reedy weak equivalence is a weak equivalence in the Segal space model structure.

•

The
complete Segal space model structure
is the left Bousfeld localization of the Reedy model structure at the set
S
of spine inclusions together with the inclusion
\{0\}\hookrightarrow I
, where
I
is the (nerve of the) walking isomorphism. The complete Segal space model structure has the following properties.

\circ

The cofibrations are the monomorphisms.

\circ

The fibrant objects are complete Segal spaces.

\circ

Every weak equivalence in the Segal space model structure is a weak equivalence in the complete Segal space model structure.

Proposition A.1.1

(Joyal–Tierney)
.

There is a Quillen equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}^{*}:\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{\mathrm{Joyal}}\longleftrightarrow\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta^{2}}^{\mathrm{CSS}}:i_{1}^{*}}

between the Joyal model structure on simplicial sets and the complete Segal space model structure on bisimplicial spaces. Here, the functors
p_{1}^{*}
and
i_{1}^{*}
are defined as follows.

•

The functor
p_{1}^{*}
takes a simplicial set
X
to the bisimplicial space
(p_{1}^{*}X)_{mn}=X_{m}
. That is, the bisimplicial set
p_{1}^{*}X
is constant in the the vertical direction, and each row is equal to the simplicial set
X
.

•

The functor
i_{1}^{*}
takes a bisimplicial set
K
to the simplicial set
(i_{1}^{*}K)_{n}=K_{n0}
. That is, in the language of
Subsection
A.1
, the simplicial set
i_{1}^{*}K
is the ‘zeroth row’ of
K
.

Note A.1.2
.

In the notation of

For more information, the reader is directed to
[
1
]
.

A.2
Divisibility of bifunctors

In this section, we recall some key results from
[
1
]
. We refer readers there for more information.

Let
\odot\colon\mathcal{E}_{1}\times\mathcal{E}_{2}\to\mathcal{E}_{3}
be a functor of
1
-categories. We will say that
\odot
is
divisible on the left
if for each
A\in\mathcal{E}_{1}
, the functor
A\odot-
admits a right adjoint
A\backslash-
. In this case, this construction turns out also to be functorial in
A
; that is, we get a functor

-\backslash-\colon\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{E}_{3}\to\mathcal{E}_{2}.

Analogously,
\odot\colon\mathcal{E}_{1}\times\mathcal{E}_{2}\to\mathcal{E}_{3}
is
divisible on the right
if for for each
B\in\mathcal{E}_{2}
, the functor
-\odot B
admits a right adjoint
-/B
. In this case we get a of two variables

-/-\colon\mathcal{E}_{3}\times\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{E}_{1}.

Example A.2.1
.

The reader may find it helpful to keep in mind the cartesian product

-\times-\colon\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}\times\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}\to\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}.

In this case, both
A\backslash X
and
X/A
are the mapping space
X^{A}
.

If
\odot
is divisible on both sides, then there is a bijection between maps of the following types:

A\odot B\to X,\qquad A\to X/B,\qquad B\to A\backslash X.

In particular, this implies that the functors
X/-
and
-\backslash X
are mutually right adjoint.

If both
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
are finitely complete and
\mathcal{E}_{3}
is finitely cocomplete, then from a map
u\colon A\to A^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, a map
v\colon B\to B^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{E}_{2}
, and a map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathcal{E}_{3}
, we can build the following maps.

•

From the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\odot B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}\odot B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\odot B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}\odot B^{\prime}}

we get a map

u\odot^{\prime}v\colon A\odot B^{\prime}\amalg_{A\odot B}A^{\prime}\odot B\to A^{\prime}\odot B^{\prime}.

•

From the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}\backslash X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\backslash X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}\backslash Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\backslash Y}

we get a map

\langle u\backslash f\rangle\colon A^{\prime}\backslash X\to A\backslash X\times_{A\backslash Y}A^{\prime}\backslash Y

•

From the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X/B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X/B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y/B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y/B}

we get a map

\langle f/v\rangle\colon X/B^{\prime}\to X/B\times_{Y/B}Y/B^{\prime}.

Proposition A.2.2
.

With the above notation, the following are equivalent adjoint lifting problems:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\odot B^{\prime}\amalg_{A\odot B}A^{\prime}\odot B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}\odot B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X/B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X/B\times_{Y/B}Y/B^{\prime}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}\backslash X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\backslash X\times_{A\backslash Y}A^{\prime}\backslash Y}
